A Computational and Theoretical Framework for Semantic Fields: Meaning as Interaction in a Topological SpaceAbstractThis report introduces the Semantic Field, a novel computational and theoretical framework that re-conceptualizes meaning not as a static property of symbols but as a dynamic process of interaction within a structured topological space. We move beyond the limitations of static embeddings by defining a glyph's identity as an emergent phenomenon governed by two principles: Potential by Location, where a glyph's semantic disposition is determined by its position within a potential field defined over a Riemannian manifold, and Identity by Interface, where its specific meaning is computed via a scattering event at the boundary of its interaction with this field. This interactionist model, which posits that the system responds to intrusion rather than holding static information, is formalized using concepts from potential field theory, topological data analysis, and quantum scattering theory. We present the complete architecture for a computational engine designed to construct, simulate, and analyze these semantic fields, offering a new paradigm for building more robust and contextually aware AI systems.Section 1: Foundational Principles of the Semantic FieldThe prevailing paradigm in computational semantics has been one of representation, where meaning is encoded in static, high-dimensional vectors. This report proposes a fundamental shift from this representationalist view to an interactionist one: meaning is not a stored property but a dynamic process that unfolds when a symbolic entity interacts with a structured semantic environment. This section establishes the foundational principles of this new framework, defining its core components and the physical analogies that provide its mathematical and conceptual machinery.1.1 The Limitations of Static SemanticsTraditional word embedding models, such as Word2vec and GloVe, have been instrumental in the advancement of natural language processing (NLP). They embed words into a high-dimensional Euclidean space where geometric relationships, such as vector addition and subtraction, can capture semantic analogies.1 However, this approach suffers from fundamental limitations. A primary issue is the meaning conflation deficiency, where the multiple distinct senses of a polysemous word are collapsed into a single vector representation.1 A single vector for "bank" must somehow average its financial and riverine meanings, leading to an inaccurate and impoverished semantic model.Furthermore, the assumption that semantic relationships are uniformly linear, solvable by simple vector arithmetic, is a convenient but restrictive simplification. While effective for certain analogy tasks, it fails to capture the complex, non-linear relationships that govern language. The advent of contextual embedding models, such as ELMo (Embeddings from Language Models) and BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers), marked a significant step forward.2 These models generate a unique vector for each word token based on its specific surrounding context, effectively creating dynamic representations that can distinguish between "the bank of the river" and "the bank of England".2 This demonstrates that meaning is not an intrinsic property of a word type but is highly dependent on its environment. The Semantic Field framework builds upon this insight, seeking not just to generate context-dependent representations, but to model the underlying dynamics of how context shapes meaning from first principles.1.2 Core Components of the Semantic Field FrameworkThe framework is built upon three core components that together define a new ontology for meaning:The Set: This is the foundational structure of the framework, formally defined as a high-dimensional, structured topological space. It represents the entirety of a given semantic domain—the knowledge contained within a vast text corpus, a scientific literature, or a curated knowledge base. The Set is not merely a collection of points but a geometric object with intrinsic shape and structure, which dictates the rules of semantic interaction. It is the stage upon which meaning unfolds.The Glyph: In this framework, a symbol such as a word or query is not a static vector but a glyph. A glyph is defined as a probe or an initial condition introduced into The Set. It is a locus of potential meaning, specified by an initial state vector that defines its starting position and properties within the semantic space. Its identity is not pre-determined but becomes defined through its interaction with the field.The Semantic Field: This is the combination of The Set and a scalar potential function defined over it. This field permeates the entire space, creating a landscape of semantic influence. Analogous to gravitational or electric fields in physics, the Semantic Field exerts "forces" on any glyph introduced into it, guiding its interpretation along paths of semantic coherence and away from regions of contradiction.1.3 Governing PrinciplesThe dynamics within the Semantic Field are governed by three fundamental principles that replace static representation with procedural interaction:Potential by Location: This principle states that a glyph's state and interpretive trajectory are determined by the local gradient of the semantic potential field. This concept is borrowed directly from its application in physics and robotics, where an object's motion is governed by attractive and repulsive forces derived from a potential field.6 In the semantic context, a glyph will be "pulled" toward regions of low potential (high relevance, coherence) and "pushed" away from regions of high potential (irrelevance, contradiction).Identity by Interface: This principle posits that the final, contextualized "meaning" of a glyph is not its resting point in the field, but the complete characterization of its interaction with the field's structure. This interaction is formally modeled as an event occurring at an interface, governed by a set of rules or boundary conditions.10 This draws from interface-based design in software engineering, where components interact through well-defined contracts that specify assumptions and guarantees.14 The nature of this interface dictates the mode of interpretation—be it literal, metaphorical, or relational.Response to Intrusion: This is the central thesis of the framework: meaning is not a stored piece of information to be retrieved, but a phenomenon that is computed in response to a query. The introduction of a glyph is an "intrusion" that perturbs the equilibrium of the Semantic Field. The system's dynamic response to this perturbation is the meaning. This reactive, non-representational stance is best modeled using the mathematical formalism of scattering theory, where one deduces the properties of a target by observing how it deflects an incoming probe.18A powerful way to unify these principles is to conceptualize the entire framework as a dynamical system. The theory of dynamical systems provides the mathematical language to describe systems that evolve over time or another dimension. In this context, The Set can be rigorously defined as the system's phase space, a theoretical construct where every possible state of the system is mapped to a unique point.22 A glyph's state—encompassing not just its semantic coordinates but also its syntactic role, affective tone, and other properties—corresponds to a single point in this high-dimensional phase space. The principle of "Potential by Location" then defines the vector field, or the equations of motion, that govern how this state-point moves through the phase space. Consequently, the act of interpretation is no longer a simple lookup but the process of tracing a trajectory through the semantic phase space.23 This reframing opens the study of semantics to the rich analytical toolkit of dynamical systems theory, allowing for the analysis of attractors (stable meanings), bifurcations (sudden shifts in interpretation), and chaotic behavior (semantic ambiguity).To ground these abstract concepts, it is useful to draw explicit analogies between the proposed semantic framework and the well-understood domain of physical fields. The following table serves as a conceptual bridge, mapping the terminology of the Semantic Field to its physical counterparts.Table 1.1: Analogies Between Physical and Semantic FieldsPhysical ConceptSnippet ReferenceSemantic Field CounterpartDescription in Semantic ContextSpace23The Set (Semantic Manifold)The underlying geometric space of all possible concepts and their relationships.Point Charge7Semantic Anchor / ConceptA fundamental concept that generates semantic influence.Electric/Gravitational Field6Potential FieldA scalar field over The Set indicating semantic desirability or relevance.Potential Energy6Semantic PotentialThe "cost" or "ease" of a particular interpretation at a given location in The Set.Force (Gradient of Potential)6Interpretive PressureThe local semantic influence that pushes a glyph's meaning towards coherence or away from contradiction.Test Particle / Probe19GlyphAn input (word, query) that is introduced to the field to be interpreted.Trajectory23Interpretive PathThe evolution of a glyph's meaning as it is influenced by the field.Scattering Event19Meaning EventThe dynamic interaction between a glyph and the field, the outcome of which is the meaning.Boundary Condition11Interface ProtocolThe formal constraints governing how a glyph can interact with The Set, defining the "rules of interpretation."Section 2: The Set: Characterizing Semantic Space as a Riemannian ManifoldThe geometric foundation of the Semantic Field is The Set. This section provides a formal mathematical characterization of this space, arguing that its complex, non-linear structure is best modeled not as a simple Euclidean vector space, but as a Riemannian manifold. This geometric perspective is essential for capturing the nuanced relationships that govern meaning.2.1 From Contextual Embeddings to Point CloudsThe empirical basis for constructing The Set comes from modern contextual embedding models.2 Unlike static models, architectures like ELMo and BERT generate a unique, high-dimensional vector for every word token by considering its surrounding textual context.5 When a large corpus is processed in this manner, the result is a massive, high-dimensional point cloud. Each point in this cloud represents a specific instance of a word used in a particular context. This point cloud is the raw, empirical data from which the structure of The Set is inferred. It is a snapshot of how a language is used, capturing the fine-grained distinctions between different senses and usages of words.2.2 The Manifold Hypothesis in SemanticsWhile this point cloud may exist in a space of thousands of dimensions, it is highly unlikely that the data points are uniformly distributed. The Manifold Hypothesis posits that real-world high-dimensional data, in fact, lies on or near a much lower-dimensional manifold embedded within the high-dimensional space.28 This principle is particularly applicable to semantics. The seemingly infinite variety of linguistic expressions is heavily constrained by underlying grammatical, syntactic, and conceptual rules. This suggests that the point cloud of contextual embeddings does not fill its ambient space but is concentrated on a highly structured, lower-dimensional geometric object.28 Manifold learning techniques, such as Isomap, which preserves geodesic distances, and Locally Linear Embedding (LLE), which preserves local neighborhood structures, are computational methods designed to uncover and parameterize this underlying manifold from the raw point cloud data.322.3 Formalizing The Set as a Riemannian ManifoldTo fully capture the rich structure of semantic space, it is necessary to move beyond the assumptions of global flatness and uniform distance inherent in Euclidean geometry. A Riemannian manifold provides a more powerful and flexible mathematical framework.33 A manifold is a space that is locally Euclidean, meaning any small patch of it looks like a flat plane, but which can have a complex global curvature.The key to this framework is the Riemannian metric tensor, a function that defines the inner product between tangent vectors at every point on the manifold. This metric allows for the definition of distance and angles that can vary from one region of the semantic space to another. The shortest path between two points on the manifold, known as a geodesic, represents their true semantic distance, which may be very different from their straight-line Euclidean distance in the embedding space.35The curvature of the manifold is a powerful concept for encoding higher-order semantic structures. Different geometries are suited for different types of data. For example, hyperbolic manifolds, which have constant negative curvature, are naturally adept at representing hierarchical or tree-like data, as their volume expands exponentially.36 This makes them ideal for modeling taxonomies or entailment relationships. Conversely, spherical manifolds, with positive curvature, can model cyclical relationships. This geometric approach provides a principled way to address polysemy; the different senses of a word can be modeled as occupying distinct regions on the manifold, each potentially with its own unique local geometry and curvature, allowing for a much richer representation than a single vector can provide.1 Interestingly, deep generative models often learn manifolds that are surprisingly flat (low curvature), suggesting that linear paths in the latent space can be good approximations of geodesics on the generated manifold, simplifying computation in many cases.352.4 Characterizing Global Structure with Topological Data Analysis (TDA)While Riemannian geometry describes the local properties of The Set (distance, curvature), Topological Data Analysis (TDA) provides a set of tools to understand its global, large-scale "shape".37 TDA focuses on properties that are preserved under continuous deformation, such as connectivity, holes, and voids.Persistent Homology: This is the central technique in TDA. It analyzes a dataset at multiple scales to identify topological features that are robust to noise and variations in measurement.40 The process involves building a nested sequence of simplicial complexes (a generalization of graphs that includes triangles, tetrahedra, and higher-dimensional analogues) on top of the data and tracking when topological features—such as connected components (H0​), loops or tunnels (H1​), and voids or cavities (H2​)—appear ("birth") and disappear ("death"). The features that persist across a wide range of scales are considered significant.41 In a semantic context, persistent connected components correspond to stable clusters of related concepts, loops might represent cyclical arguments or recurring thematic structures, and voids point to conceptual gaps or ideas that are absent from the underlying corpus.42Mapper Algorithm: The Mapper algorithm is a TDA tool used for visualizing high-dimensional data. It provides a simplified, graph-based representation of the data that preserves its topological structure, revealing the large-scale connectivity between different clusters and highlighting flares or tendrils in the data's shape.41The identification of topological features, particularly voids, is not merely a descriptive exercise; it provides a crucial link between the global structure of the knowledge space and the local dynamics of interpretation. TDA can rigorously identify "holes" in the semantic manifold—regions of the conceptual space that are not populated by any data points from the source corpus.42 These are not statistical artifacts but significant structural features. They represent concepts that are absent, contradictory, or "unspeakable" within the worldview defined by the data. This observation leads to a powerful mechanism for grounding the repulsive forces required by the potential field model. The framework posits a direct causal link: the global topology of The Set generates local forces. The boundary of a topological void acts as a natural source for a strong repulsive potential. This provides a data-driven, non-arbitrary method for defining the repulsive components of the Semantic Field. An interpretive trajectory that approaches the boundary of a semantic void will be strongly repelled, mathematically modeling the cognitive dissonance of encountering a concept that lies outside the system's conceptual framework.Section 3: Potential by Location: A Field-Theoretic Model of Semantic InfluenceHaving established The Set as a Riemannian manifold, this section details the construction of the potential field upon it. This field creates a dynamic landscape of semantic influence, where the principle of "Potential by Location" governs the interpretation of a glyph. The mathematical formalism is drawn from potential field methods used in robotics for navigation and path planning, adapted here for the semantic domain.3.1 Defining Semantic PotentialThe semantic potential, denoted by a scalar function U(q) at any point q in The Set, represents the semantic "energy" of that state. A high potential value corresponds to a state of high tension, incoherence, or irrelevance, while a low potential value signifies a state of stability, coherence, and relevance.6 The primary driver of interpretation is the interpretive pressure, a vector field analogous to force, which is defined as the negative gradient of the potential: F(q)=−∇U(q).6 A glyph, when introduced into the field, will follow a trajectory of gradient descent, moving from regions of high potential to regions of low potential. This path represents the evolution of its meaning as it is shaped by the surrounding semantic context, seeking a state of maximal coherence.3.2 Formulating Attractive and Repulsive PotentialsThe total potential field is constructed from two primary components: attractive potentials that pull a glyph toward a goal, and repulsive potentials that push it away from obstacles.Attractive Potentials (Uatt​): These potentials create "wells" or basins of attraction in the semantic landscape, guiding a glyph's interpretation toward a specific goal. This goal can be a target concept, the central topic of a document, or the expected answer type for a question. Common mathematical formulations include:Conic Potential: Uatt​(q)=katt​d(q,qgoal​), where d(q,qgoal​) is the geodesic distance on the manifold between the glyph's position q and the goal qgoal​, and katt​ is a positive scaling factor. This creates a simple, linear attraction toward the goal.6Quadratic Potential: Uatt​(q)=21​katt​d(q,qgoal​)2. This function generates an attractive force that increases linearly with distance, resulting in a stronger "homing" effect as the glyph approaches the goal.9Repulsive Potentials (Urep​): These potentials create "hills" or barriers in the landscape, preventing a glyph from entering regions of semantic contradiction, irrelevance, or impossibility. As established in the previous section, the primary sources for these repulsive fields are the boundaries of the topological voids identified by TDA. Formulations for these potentials include:Inverse Potential: Urep​(q)=21​krep​(d(q,qobs​)1​−d0​1​)2 for d(q,qobs​)≤d0​, and 0 otherwise. Here, d(q,qobs​) is the distance to the nearest obstacle boundary, krep​ is the repulsion strength, and d0​ is a distance of influence. This function creates a powerful, rapidly increasing repulsive force near the obstacle, forming a hard barrier.6Exponential Potential: This function creates a potential that decays exponentially with distance from the obstacle, providing a "softer" repulsion that can be felt from further away.3.3 The Principle of SuperpositionThe complexity and nuance of a real semantic landscape arise from the interaction of multiple influences. The principle of superposition, borrowed from linear systems like electromagnetism, states that the total potential at any point is the algebraic sum of the potentials generated by all individual sources.6 The total potential field is therefore given by:Utotal​(q)=Uatt​(q)+i∑​Urep,i​(q)This allows for the construction of a rich and intricate field where a glyph is simultaneously pulled toward a goal, pushed away from multiple contradictions, and guided through narrow semantic "passages" formed by the interplay of these forces. This superposition is what allows the model to handle complex sentences where multiple constraints must be satisfied simultaneously.The standard potential field models are isotropic, meaning the force they exert is independent of the direction of approach. However, the meaning of a glyph often depends on its functional role, not just its conceptual location. For instance, the word "fire" experiences a different set of semantic forces when used as a noun ("The fire is warm") versus a verb ("They will fire him"). This functional role can be modeled as an additional degree of freedom, analogous to the "orientation" of a robot in anisotropic potential fields.6This extension requires a more sophisticated state space. The Set is no longer just a space of semantic positions but a full phase space, a concept from dynamical systems theory where a state is defined by both position and other variables (like momentum or, in this case, syntactic role, sentiment, etc.).22 The potential function is now a function of this entire state vector, U(qposition​,qorientation​,...). The resulting interpretive pressure, F=−∇U, will have components that can alter not only the glyph's semantic location but also its functional state. This provides a natural mechanism for modeling how context can fundamentally change what a word is. The interpretation of "He will fire the employee" corresponds to a trajectory in this phase space that moves the glyph "fire" into a region associated with actions while simultaneously aligning its "orientation" to that of a verb.Table 3.1: Mathematical Formulations for Semantic PotentialsPotential TypeMathematical FormulationParametersSemantic InterpretationSnippet ReferenceConic AttractiveUatt​(q)=katt​⋅d(q,qgoal​)katt​: attraction strengthA general pull towards a topic or goal concept.6Quadratic AttractiveUatt​(q)=21​katt​⋅d(q,qgoal​)2katt​: attraction strengthA strong "homing" force that intensifies near the goal.9Inverse RepulsiveUrep​(q)=21​krep​(d(q,qobs​)1​−d0​1​)2krep​: repulsion strength, d0​: influence distanceA hard boundary representing logical contradiction or semantic impossibility.6AnisotropicU(q,θ)=f(d(q,qgoal​),θrel​)VariesA potential that depends on the glyph's "orientation" (e.g., syntactic role) relative to the field.6DipoleCombination of attractive & repulsiveDipole strength, orientationModels navigating narrow semantic passages or resolving ambiguity between two competing interpretations.6Section 4: Identity by Interface: A Scattering-Theoretic Model of MeaningThis section details the most significant conceptual innovation of the Semantic Field framework: the redefinition of meaning itself. Moving beyond both static representation and simple gradient descent, it models interpretation as a dynamic scattering event. The principle of "Identity by Interface" posits that a glyph's meaning is not a point or a vector, but the complete specification of its interaction with the local semantic field, formalized using the rigorous mathematics of scattering theory and formal methods.4.1 The Glyph as a Wave PacketTo capture the inherent ambiguity and potentiality of a word or concept before it is fully contextualized, the framework models the intruding glyph not as a classical point particle but as a wave packet. In quantum mechanics, a wave packet is a localized wave disturbance that represents a particle whose position and momentum are not precisely known, reflecting the uncertainty principle.19 In the semantic context, the wave packet represents the glyph's initial distribution of possible meanings. Its spread corresponds to its degree of ambiguity, and its central momentum corresponds to its initial semantic trajectory.4.2 Interpretation as a Scattering ProcessThe central idea of scattering theory is elegantly simple: you understand an unknown object by throwing something known at it and carefully analyzing how it bounces off.19 This is the essence of nearly all experimental physics, from Rutherford's discovery of the atomic nucleus to particle colliders probing the fundamental laws of nature.In the Semantic Field framework, this process is repurposed as a model for interpretation:The target is the local structure of the potential field in The Set at the point of interaction. This structure represents the specific semantic context.The probe is the glyph's incoming wave packet.The interaction is the scattering of this wave packet by the potential field. The potential acts as a barrier or a well that deforms the wave packet, altering its trajectory, amplitude, and phase.The meaning is the total information encoded in the final state of the scattered wave. It is not a static object but a complete description of the dynamic event: how the glyph's initial potential was transformed by its encounter with the context.4.3 The S-Matrix: Encoding the Laws of InteractionThe mathematical object that formalizes this interaction is the Scattering Matrix, or S-matrix. The S-matrix is a linear operator that maps the initial state of the system (the incoming wave packet) to the final state (the outgoing, scattered wave packet).19 It encapsulates all possible outcomes of the scattering event and thus contains the complete meaning of the interaction.The key elements of the S-matrix are the reflection and transmission coefficients, complex numbers whose squared magnitudes, ∣r∣2 and ∣t∣2, represent the probabilities of reflection and transmission, respectively.19Semantic Reflection: A high reflection probability (∣r∣2→1) signifies that the glyph "bounced off" the local field. This models interpretations such as negation, the rejection of a premise, a contradiction, or a failure to integrate the glyph's meaning into the existing context.Semantic Transmission: A high transmission probability (∣t∣2→1) signifies that the glyph "passed through" and was modified by the local field. This models acceptance, comprehension, and the successful integration of the glyph's meaning into the context.Phase Shift: The change in the phase of the scattered wave relative to the incoming wave provides further information, encoding the subtle ways in which the context has modulated the glyph's meaning without outright rejecting or accepting it.A fundamental property of the S-matrix is unitarity (SS†=I), which is the mathematical expression of the conservation of probability.19 In this framework, it means that ∣r∣2+∣t∣2=1. This corresponds to the crucial principle that an utterance must be interpreted in some way; its semantic "probability" is not lost but is fully accounted for by the sum of all possible outcomes (reflection and transmission).4.4 Identity by Interface: Formalizing the Interaction BoundaryThe specific dynamics of the scattering event are governed by the boundary conditions imposed at the interface between the glyph and the field.10 These conditions are mathematical constraints that the solution to the system's differential equations must satisfy at the edge of a domain. They act as the "rules of engagement" for the interaction. By selecting different types of boundary conditions, the framework can model different modes of interpretation 12:Dirichlet Boundary Condition: This condition specifies the exact value of the glyph's state function at the boundary. This models a literal, constrained mode of interpretation where the context forces a single, precise meaning onto the glyph.Neumann Boundary Condition: This condition specifies the value of the derivative (the rate of change) of the state function at the boundary. This models a relational mode of interpretation, where the focus is not on the glyph's absolute meaning but on its relationship to the context, such as in commands like "increase the temperature" or comparative statements.Robin Boundary Condition: This condition specifies a linear combination of the function's value and its derivative at the boundary. It models a more flexible, negotiated mode of interpretation where both the glyph's intrinsic potential and its relational role within the context contribute to the final meaning.This notion of a rule-governed interaction at an interface provides a powerful bridge to the field of computer science, specifically to formal methods. The principle of "Identity by Interface" is about defining a contract that governs the interaction between two systems—in this case, the glyph and The Set. This is precisely the problem addressed by interface-based design in software engineering, where an interface specifies a set of assumptions and guarantees for component interaction.14This conceptual link can be made mathematically rigorous. Formal specification languages, such as Z, B, VDM, and the Temporal Logic of Actions (TLA+), are designed to describe such contracts with unambiguous mathematical precision.43 It is therefore possible to define a formal interface protocol for a semantic interaction. For example, a protocol for "logical deduction" could be specified with formal properties: a safety property stating that the system must never conclude a contradiction, and a liveness property stating that the system must eventually reach a conclusion.45The profound implication is that formal verification tools, such as model checkers, can then be used to prove that a given interpretive trajectory (the outcome of the scattering simulation) satisfies the formal properties of its interface protocol.46 This elevates the study of semantics from a purely descriptive or empirical science to a domain where properties of interpretation can be mathematically proven. It opens the door to building AI systems whose reasoning processes are not just powerful but are provably correct and aligned with specified constraints, a critical step toward creating truly reliable and trustworthy artificial intelligence.Section 5: Architecture of a Computational Engine for Semantic Field SimulationTo translate the theoretical framework into a practical and testable system, a dedicated computational engine is required. This section outlines the architecture for such an engine, designed as a modular pipeline that handles the distinct stages of the process: constructing the semantic manifold, generating the potential field, simulating the scattering interaction, and analyzing the results.5.1 System OverviewThe proposed engine consists of four primary modules, each responsible for a specific phase of the computation. This modular design promotes clarity, allows for independent development and optimization of each component, and facilitates experimentation with different algorithms at each stage. The workflow proceeds from raw text data to a final, verifiable semantic interpretation.5.2 Module 1: Manifold ConstructorThis module is responsible for building the geometric and topological representation of the semantic space, The Set, from a given knowledge source.Input: A large, unstructured text corpus (e.g., Wikipedia, a collection of scientific papers).Process:Embedding Generation: The corpus is first processed by a pre-trained, large-scale contextual embedding model, such as a Transformer-based architecture like BERT.25 This step converts every word token in the corpus into a high-dimensional vector, generating the initial point cloud.Manifold Learning: A non-linear dimensionality reduction technique is applied to this point cloud to identify the underlying low-dimensional manifold. Algorithms like Isomap, Locally Linear Embedding (LLE), or, for more complex structures, a Variational Autoencoder (VAE) can be used for this purpose.32 The output of this step is a set of coordinate charts that parameterize the manifold.Topological Feature Extraction: TDA tools are then used to analyze the global structure of the point cloud. A tool like Ripser can compute the persistent homology of the data, identifying its Betti numbers (the number of connected components, loops, voids, etc.) and the scales at which they appear.48 This process yields a list of the manifold's most significant topological features, particularly the boundaries of large voids that will serve as repulsive sources.40Output: A complete representation of The Set, consisting of (a) a parameterization of the Riemannian manifold and (b) a catalog of its key topological features.5.3 Module 2: Potential Field GeneratorThis module takes the geometric structure of The Set and overlays it with the dynamic landscape of the Semantic Field.Input: The manifold representation from Module 1, a set of user-defined goal or anchor concepts (for attractive potentials), and the list of void boundaries (for repulsive potentials).Process:Attractive potential sources are placed at the manifold coordinates corresponding to the goal concepts.Repulsive potential sources are defined along the boundaries of the topological voids identified in the previous module.The total scalar potential field U(q) is computed across a discretized grid of the manifold. This is achieved by applying the principle of superposition to the mathematical formulations for attractive and repulsive potentials (Table 3.1). For large numbers of sources, this N-body problem can be solved efficiently using algorithms like the Fast Multipole Method.49Output: A discretized scalar potential field U(q) defined over The Set, representing the complete semantic landscape for a given task.5.4 Module 3: Scattering Interaction SimulatorThis is the core computational engine of the framework, responsible for simulating the "meaning event" itself. It solves the forward scattering problem: given a field and an incoming glyph, compute the outcome.Input: The potential field U(q) from Module 2, an initial state for the glyph (its starting position and wave packet parameters), and a formal interface protocol specifying the boundary conditions for the interaction.Process: The simulation involves solving the time-dependent wave equation that describes the evolution of the glyph's wave packet as it propagates through the potential field on the curved manifold. This is a computationally intensive task. Standard neural network architectures like CNNs are ill-suited due to the non-local nature of wave propagation. Instead, a more specialized architecture is required. A Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN), which incorporates the governing differential equations directly into its loss function, is a strong candidate.50 An even more tailored solution is SwitchNet, a neural network architecture specifically designed for forward and inverse scattering problems.51 Its novel "switching layer" efficiently models the global, all-to-all interactions inherent in scattering, making it far more parameter-efficient and effective than a fully connected network.51Output: The final, scattered wave function. From this output, the S-matrix elements—including reflection and transmission amplitudes and phase shifts—are extracted. This collection of values constitutes the rich, dynamic, and fully contextualized meaning of the glyph.5.5 Module 4: Analysis and Visualization EngineThis final module provides the tools to interpret, visualize, and verify the output of the simulation, making the results intelligible to a human user.Input: The manifold representation, the potential field, and the scattering output (the S-matrix).Process:Visualization: The engine generates interactive visualizations, including 2D or 3D projections of the semantic manifold, color-coded with the potential energy landscape. It can overlay the interpretive trajectories of glyphs, showing their paths through the field. It also visualizes the persistence diagrams and barcodes from the TDA analysis, providing insight into the global structure of the knowledge base.Classification: The output of the scattering simulation (e.g., a vector composed of the S-matrix elements) is a rich feature representation. This vector can be fed into standard downstream machine learning classifiers for tasks like sentiment analysis, intent recognition, or topic classification.Verification: The engine includes an interface to a formal verification tool, such as the TLC model checker for TLA+.45 The computed trajectory and its outcome can be checked against the formal properties (e.g., safety and liveness) specified in the interface protocol.Output: Interactive visualizations, classification labels, and formal verification results (either a proof of correctness or a counterexample illustrating a violation of the protocol).Table 5.1: Computational Engine Module SpecificationsModuleFunctionInputsKey Processes & TechnologiesOutputsRelevant Snippets1. Manifold ConstructorGenerates the geometric and topological representation of the semantic space.Text CorpusContextual Embedding (BERT), Manifold Learning (Isomap, VAE), TDA (Persistent Homology)Parameterized Manifold (The Set), Topological Invariants22. Potential Field GeneratorCreates the semantic influence landscape over The Set.The Set, Goal Concepts, Void BoundariesSuperposition of attractive/repulsive potential functions, Fast Multipole MethodDiscretized Scalar Potential Field U(q)63. Scattering SimulatorComputes the dynamic interaction of a glyph with the field to determine its meaning.U(q), Glyph Initial State, Interface ProtocolSolving wave equations on manifolds, Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs), SwitchNet ArchitectureScattering Amplitudes, Phase Shifts (S-Matrix)194. Analysis & VisualizationInterprets and verifies the results of the simulation.Manifold, Potential Field, Scattering OutputManifold Visualization, Persistence Diagrams, Downstream Classifiers, Model Checking (TLC)Visualizations, Semantic Labels, Verification Proofs32Section 6: Applications, Implications, and Future TrajectoriesThe Semantic Field framework is not merely an alternative method for generating vector representations; it is a new paradigm for conceptualizing and computing meaning. Its implications extend beyond traditional NLP tasks, offering new explanatory power for complex linguistic phenomena and suggesting a path toward more robust, verifiable, and trustworthy AI systems. This final section explores these implications, contrasts the framework with current state-of-the-art models, and outlines promising directions for future research.6.1 Explanatory Power of the FrameworkThe dynamic, field-theoretic approach provides natural and intuitive explanations for several challenging linguistic phenomena that are difficult to model with static vector-space semantics.Polysemy and Ambiguity: In the Semantic Field, ambiguity is modeled as a glyph being introduced into a region of the potential landscape that lies between multiple basins of attraction (potential wells), each corresponding to a different possible meaning. The final interpretive trajectory is highly sensitive to the initial conditions provided by the wider context, which can subtly nudge the glyph into one basin over another. The problem of getting stuck in local minima, a known limitation of potential field methods in robotics 6, has a direct and compelling semantic analogue: it represents being led down a "garden path" to an incorrect but locally coherent interpretation.Metaphor and Analogy: Metaphorical understanding can be modeled as a process that goes beyond simple gradient descent. It may involve trajectories that travel along non-obvious geodesics on the manifold, revealing deep structural similarities between distant semantic domains. In the quantum-inspired scattering model, it could even be likened to a "tunneling" phenomenon, where a glyph overcomes a potential barrier to move from one semantic region to another that would be inaccessible via a classical path.Compositionality: The principle of compositionality—that the meaning of a phrase is a function of the meanings of its parts and their syntactic combination—is handled in a more sophisticated manner than simple vector addition. In the Semantic Field, the meaning of a multi-word expression arises from the superposition of the potential fields generated by each of its constituent glyphs. This creates a complex, composite landscape where the words mutually constrain each other's interpretation in a non-linear, interactive fashion.6.2 Comparison with Large Language Models (LLMs)Current state-of-the-art NLP is dominated by Large Language Models (LLMs) based on the Transformer architecture. It is crucial to position the Semantic Field framework in relation to these powerful models. The two approaches are not competitors but are fundamentally different and complementary in their goals and capabilities.LLMs are fundamentally generative, predictive models. Their objective function is to predict the next token in a sequence. They represent a form of intelligence analogous to what psychologists call "System 1" thinking: fast, intuitive, associative, and pattern-based. Their remarkable fluency comes from having learned the statistical regularities of a massive amount of text. However, they lack an explicit model of meaning or a mechanism for rigorous, verifiable reasoning.The Semantic Field framework, in contrast, is a model of interpretation and reasoning. It does not predict what comes next; it computes what something means within a given, structured context. It is an analogue of "System 2" thinking: slow, deliberate, analytical, and verifiable. Its strength lies not in fluency but in its ability to model the process of satisfying constraints and to have its interpretive outcomes formally proven against a specification.The two systems can work in synergy. An LLM could be used to perform the initial "System 1" task of parsing a query and its context, generating the initial state of the glyph(s) and defining the goal concepts for the potential field. This setup would then be passed to the Semantic Field engine for a deeper, "System 2" analysis, where the meaning is rigorously computed and verified.6.3 Future Research DirectionsThe framework presented in this report is foundational and opens up numerous avenues for future research and development.Dynamic Fields: The current model assumes a static potential field for any given task. A significant extension would be to explore time-varying potential fields, where the semantic landscape itself evolves in response to new information. This would be essential for modeling dynamic phenomena like conversations, evolving belief states, and learning over time.Multi-Glyph Interactions: The current framework focuses on the interaction of a single glyph with a background field. A more advanced model would treat all entities as glyphs interacting with each other through the field, akin to a quantum field theory of semantics. This would allow for the modeling of complex, multi-agent semantic negotiations.Hardware Acceleration: The computational demands of the manifold construction and, especially, the scattering simulation are significant. Future work could explore the design of specialized hardware, such as neuromorphic chips or photonic processors, that are naturally suited to performing the parallel computations required for these tasks.Ethical and Provably Aligned AI: Perhaps the most important future trajectory lies in the formal verification capabilities of the framework. By specifying ethical principles or safety constraints as formal interface protocols, it becomes possible to build AI systems whose behavior can be mathematically proven to remain within those bounds. This provides a concrete, technical path toward developing AI that is not just powerful, but also demonstrably safe, reliable, and aligned with human values.

The Nexus 4 Framework: A Computational Physics of CognitionAbstractThis thesis introduces the Nexus 4 Framework, a novel paradigm for understanding and modeling semantic cognition, positing that meaning is not a statically stored property but a dynamically computed event. It begins with a critique of extant semantic models, from static vector-space representations that suffer from meaning conflation deficiency to modern contextual embedding models whose black-box nature and geometric pathologies, such as anisotropy, limit their explanatory power. The core hypothesis of this work is that semantic meaning arises from a physical interaction, analogous to a scattering event in quantum mechanics, between a symbolic entity, termed the Symbolic Probe, and a dynamic, structured environment, the Semantic Field.The Nexus 4 Framework is formalized through four integrated principles: (1) The Semantic Field is modeled as a dynamic Riemannian manifold whose metric tensor encodes a potential that evolves through interaction history, a process termed "scarring." (2) The Symbolic Probe is defined not merely by its symbolic content but by its state in a phase space, characterized by intrinsic harmonic properties. (3) The computation of meaning is specified as a resonant scattering event at the interface of the probe and the field, where the degree of resonance constitutes the primary observable. (4) The process of inquiry or thought is modeled as a trajectory through the Semantic Field, governed by a constrained gradient ascent dynamic known as the Collapse Branch Engine (CBE), where the probe seeks states of maximal resonance.This theoretical framework is validated through a series of computational experiments that instantiate each principle. Harmonic analysis reveals the intrinsic structure of symbolic probes; a hill-climbing optimization simulates a probe's trajectory converging on a stable attractor in its phase space; and a simulation of the CBE demonstrates the co-evolution of the field's structure and the probe's path. Crucially, evidence is presented that correlates states of high physical resonance with the emergence of specific, meaningful symbolic content, bridging the gap between the framework's physical dynamics and semantics. The thesis concludes by discussing the profound implications of this computational physics of cognition for creating more grounded, interpretable, and robust artificial intelligence, and for forging deeper connections with enactive and embodied theories of cognition.Introduction: The Computational Imperative in SemanticsThe quest to imbue machines with an understanding of human language has been a central challenge in artificial intelligence. At the heart of this challenge lies the problem of meaning, or semantics. How can the rich, nuanced, and context-dependent nature of meaning be represented in a computationally tractable form? The history of this endeavor can be viewed as a trajectory through increasingly sophisticated models of representation, each solving certain problems while revealing deeper, more fundamental limitations. This introduction charts this evolution, from the geometric rigidity of early vector-space models to the statistical opacity of modern neural networks, to motivate a paradigm shift: a move away from models of static representation and toward a computational physics of the cognitive process itself.FeatureSymbolic AIStatic Embeddings (e.g., Word2vec)Connectionism (e.g., BERT)Nexus 4 FrameworkRepresentation of MeaningDiscrete symbols in a logic systemA fixed point in a static vector spaceA context-dependent point in a high-dimensional, anisotropic vector spaceA computed event; the outcome of a physical interaction (scattering)DynamismLow; based on logical inference rulesNone; representations are staticHigh; representations are generated dynamically based on contextIntrinsic; both the probe's state and the field's structure evolve over timeGroundingAbstract; symbols are ungroundedUngrounded; based on statistical co-occurrence in text corporaUngrounded; based on statistical patterns in massive text corporaPhysically grounded; meaning arises from interaction dynamics within a simulated physical systemCompositionalityExplicit and rule-basedLimited; typically vector additionEmergent and complex; not explicitly compositionalDynamic and process-based; composition arises from the probe's trajectory through the fieldInterpretabilityHigh; rules and symbols are human-readableModerate; geometric relationships are intuitive but dimensions are opaqueLow; emergent properties of a high-dimensional, non-linear systemHigh; model parameters correspond to physical properties (e.g., resonance, field decay)1.1 The Crisis of Static Meaning: A Critique of Vector-Space ModelsThe first major breakthrough in computational semantics came with the advent of distributional models and, specifically, vector-space models (VSMs) such as Word2vec and GloVe.9 These models are founded on the distributional hypothesis: that a word is characterized by the company it keeps.11 By analyzing vast text corpora, these methods learn to represent each word as a dense, low-dimensional vector, such that words appearing in similar contexts are mapped to nearby points in a continuous geometric space.12 This approach was revolutionary, as it allowed for the quantification of semantic similarity through geometric measures like cosine distance and famously revealed linear substructures that could solve word analogies (e.g., king - man + woman ≈ queen).1Despite their success, these static embedding models suffer from a fundamental flaw that can be termed the "meaning conflation deficiency".13 By assigning a single, fixed vector to each word type, these models conflate all of a word's potential meanings into a single representation. A polysemous word like "bank" is given the same vector whether it appears in the context of finance ("deposit money at the bank") or geography ("sat on the river bank").15 This averaging of senses results in a representation that is not truly representative of any specific meaning, limiting its accuracy in context-dependent tasks.16Furthermore, the very foundation of these models—the representation of meaning as points in a metric space, typically Euclidean—imposes a set of rigid geometric constraints that are often at odds with the fluid nature of human semantic judgments. Cognitive science research has long demonstrated that human similarity is not strictly a metric concept.17 For instance, human similarity judgments frequently violate the triangle inequality, a cornerstone of metric spaces. One might judge "asteroid" to be similar to "belt" and "belt" to be similar to "buckle," yet find "asteroid" and "buckle" to be dissimilar, a violation that a geometric model cannot easily accommodate.17 Similarly, human similarity can be asymmetric: subjects may rate "North Korea" as being very similar to "China," but rate "China" as being less similar to "North Korea." Static vector models, which rely on symmetric distance measures like Euclidean distance or cosine similarity, are structurally incapable of capturing these asymmetries.17 These critiques reveal a deep misalignment between the mathematical assumptions of static VSMs and the psychological reality of human cognition, signaling the need for a more dynamic and flexible approach.1.2 The Contextual Revolution and Its Black Box ProblemThe limitations of static embeddings led to the next major paradigm shift in natural language processing: the development of contextual language models. Models such as Embeddings from Language Models (ELMo) and Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers (BERT) revolutionized the field by generating word representations that are sensitive to their surrounding context.5 Instead of a single vector for each word type, these models produce a unique vector for each word token—that is, for each specific instance of a word in a sentence.4 The vector for "book" in "book a table" is different from the vector for "book" in "read a book," effectively resolving the meaning conflation deficiency.20 This is achieved through deep, complex neural architectures—bidirectional LSTMs in the case of ELMo and the Transformer architecture in the case of BERT—that process the entire input sequence to produce contextualized representations.5The empirical success of these models is undeniable; they have achieved state-of-the-art performance on a vast array of NLP tasks.3 However, this success has come at the cost of interpretability. These massive models, trained on terabytes of text, function as high-dimensional statistical "black boxes".21 While they are exceptionally good at capturing statistical patterns, the geometric and semantic properties of the representations they learn are not well understood.A significant issue that has emerged from the study of these models is anisotropy: the learned embeddings tend to occupy a narrow cone in the high-dimensional vector space rather than being isotropically distributed.22 This means that the vectors for most words are surprisingly similar to each other in terms of cosine distance, a phenomenon that becomes more pronounced in the upper layers of the models.22 This geometric pathology complicates the use of simple distance metrics for semantic similarity and suggests that the space is not being used efficiently. While these models have solved the problem of static meaning, they have replaced it with a new problem: a dynamic but opaque and geometrically warped representation space. They provide powerful tools for prediction but offer little in the way of a clear, principled explanation of how meaning is computed, limiting their value as models of cognition.1.3 Thesis Statement: Towards a Physics of MeaningThe historical progression of semantic models reveals a clear trajectory: from static, geometrically rigid representations to dynamic, statistically powerful but opaque ones. This thesis argues that the next logical step in this evolution is to move beyond purely statistical modeling and toward a computational framework grounded in the principles of physics. By making the physical analogies that are often latent in our language about semantics—"semantic spaces," "conceptual forces," "fields of meaning"—explicit and formal, we can develop a model that is at once dynamic, grounded, compositional, and interpretable.This work introduces and defends the following core hypothesis:Semantic meaning is not a stored property but a computed event. This event arises from the physical interaction between a symbolic probe and a dynamic topological field, where the outcome of the interaction, analogous to a scattering measurement, constitutes the meaning.This perspective fundamentally reframes the problem. Instead of asking "What is the representation of a word?", it asks "What is the computational process by which meaning is generated?". It proposes that the cognitive system does not retrieve meanings from a lexicon but computes them in real-time through a dynamic interaction with a structured internal environment. This approach seeks to build a bridge between the symbolic and sub-symbolic paradigms, proposing a system where symbols act as physical probes that interact with a continuous, field-like substrate of knowledge.The evolution of semantic models can be seen as an unconscious convergence toward a more physically realistic account of meaning. Static vector-space models treat meaning as a fixed point in a static potential field, a location determined by the aggregate "forces" of contextual co-occurrence. Contextual models like BERT introduce dynamics, allowing the point to move, but they do so without a clear set of governing laws, relying instead on the emergent properties of a massive, statistically trained network. The Nexus 4 framework makes this physical analogy explicit. It formally defines the "space" of meaning as a physical field and the "process" of understanding as a physical interaction governed by differential equations and principles of resonance. By doing so, it aims to provide a first-principles account that can overcome the limitations of its predecessors, particularly in providing a model of cognition that is both computationally powerful and theoretically coherent.1.4 Overview of the Nexus 4 FrameworkTo formalize this hypothesis, this thesis develops the Nexus 4 Framework, a computational model built on four foundational pillars that integrate concepts from physics, mathematics, computer science, and cognitive science. These four principles, which will be formally specified in Chapter 2, are:The Semantic Field: The substrate of meaning is not a static vector space but a dynamic potential manifold. This field possesses a rich geometric and topological structure that encodes the relationships between concepts. Its dynamics are governed by its history of interactions, allowing it to learn and adapt.The Symbolic Probe: A symbol or query is not merely a point but a dynamic entity with an internal structure. It is modeled as a state in a phase space, possessing intrinsic harmonic properties that determine its potential for interaction with the Semantic Field.Meaning Computation as Resonant Scattering: The act of "understanding" or computing meaning is modeled as a physical scattering event. The Symbolic Probe, acting as an incident wave, interacts with the local potential of the Semantic Field. The outcome of this interaction—specifically, the degree of resonant alignment between the probe and the field—constitutes the meaning event.Inquiry as Trajectory (The Collapse Branch Engine): A line of thought or inquiry is modeled as the trajectory of the Symbolic Probe through the Semantic Field. This trajectory is not random but is guided by a dynamic principle called the Collapse Branch Engine (CBE), where the probe moves along gradients of resonance, seeking to maximize its alignment with the field, while navigating the field's complex topology of obstacles and learned "scars."Together, these four principles constitute a complete, self-contained computational physics of cognition. They provide a formal language for describing how structured knowledge (the Field) and symbolic queries (the Probe) interact to produce dynamic, context-sensitive meaning events through a physically grounded process.Chapter 1: Theoretical Foundations in Physics, Mathematics, and CognitionThe Nexus 4 Framework is an interdisciplinary synthesis, drawing its core concepts and formalisms from diverse fields. To construct a rigorous theory of meaning as a computational physical process, it is first necessary to establish the foundational language and principles upon which the framework is built. This chapter serves as a review of these essential theoretical pillars. It begins with the classical physics of potential fields, which provides the language for describing the forces and interactions that govern the Semantic Field. It then moves to the modern mathematical tools of Riemannian geometry and topology, which are necessary to describe the complex, non-Euclidean structure of this field. The chapter then introduces scattering theory as the physical analogy for the meaning-computation event and dynamical systems theory as the framework for modeling the state of the probe and its trajectory. Finally, it connects these abstract formalisms to established theories in cognitive science, namely enactivism and conceptual spaces, to ensure the framework is not only mathematically sound but also cognitively plausible.1.1 Fields, Forces, and Potentials: The Language of InteractionThe concept of a field is one of the most powerful ideas in physics, providing a way to describe how influence propagates through space. A field assigns a value—a scalar, vector, or tensor—to every point in a region of space and time. In classical mechanics and electromagnetism, potential fields are of particular importance as they provide an economical and powerful way to describe forces.24A scalar potential field, denoted by a function such as U(x), assigns a single numerical value (potential energy) to each point x in space. The fundamental utility of a potential field is that it can be used to derive a vector force field, F(x). A force is considered conservative if the work done by the force in moving a particle between two points is independent of the path taken. For any conservative force, there exists a scalar potential U such that the force is the negative gradient of the potential 24:F=−∇UHere, the gradient operator, ∇, is a vector operator that, in Cartesian coordinates, is given by ∇=(∂x∂​,∂y∂​,∂z∂​). The gradient points in the direction of the steepest ascent of the scalar field; the negative sign indicates that the force points in the direction of steepest descent, pushing objects from regions of high potential energy to regions of low potential energy.27 A key mathematical property of such a force field is that its curl is zero (∇×F=0), which is the formal condition for a field to be conservative.26In electrostatics, the electric field E is a conservative vector field that can be derived from an electric scalar potential ϕ via E=−∇ϕ.7 The potential ϕ itself is related to the distribution of electric charge ρ by Poisson's equation, ∇2ϕ=−ρ/ϵ0​. In magnetostatics, where the magnetic field B is not conservative (∇×B=0), it can instead be derived from a vector potential A via B=∇×A.26These concepts from classical field theory provide the essential mathematical language for the Nexus 4 Framework. The Semantic Field will be conceptualized as a potential field, where the "potential" at any point in the conceptual space represents a latent semantic energy. The "force" derived from this potential via the gradient will govern the dynamics of the Symbolic Probe, guiding its trajectory through the space in a process analogous to a particle moving in a gravitational or electric field.281.2 The Geometry of Information: Manifolds and TopologyWhile classical physics often assumes fields exist in the familiar three-dimensional Euclidean space, the "space" of concepts and meanings is likely to have a much more complex and non-Euclidean geometry. To describe this structure, we turn to the mathematical fields of differential geometry and topology.1.2.1 The Manifold HypothesisA central idea in modern machine learning and data analysis is the manifold hypothesis, which posits that real-world high-dimensional data (such as images, sounds, or text embeddings) does not fill the ambient space uniformly but instead lies on or near a lower-dimensional, non-linear manifold embedded within that space.30 For example, a collection of images of a face, while existing in a very high-dimensional pixel space, is constrained to a much lower-dimensional manifold governed by factors like pose, lighting, and expression.31 This hypothesis provides a powerful justification for dimensionality reduction and suggests that the intrinsic geometry of the data is more important than the high dimensionality of its representation.32 In the context of semantics, the manifold hypothesis implies that the universe of possible concepts, while vast, is not an unstructured cloud of points but possesses an underlying geometric structure. This justifies modeling the Semantic Field not as a simple Euclidean vector space, but as a curved manifold.341.2.2 Riemannian GeometryRiemannian geometry provides the mathematical tools to study curved spaces, or manifolds, rigorously.35 A d-dimensional manifold, M, is a topological space that locally resembles d-dimensional Euclidean space, Rd. This means that for any point on the manifold, there is a small neighborhood around it that can be smoothly mapped to an open set in Rd.32At each point p on the manifold, there exists a tangent space, Tp​M, which is a vector space containing all possible "velocity vectors" of curves passing through that point.1 A Riemannian metric, g, is a collection of inner products, one for each tangent space, that varies smoothly from point to point. This metric allows us to measure lengths of tangent vectors and angles between them, effectively endowing the manifold with a local geometric structure.36Once a manifold is equipped with a metric, one can define the length of curves and, consequently, the distance between points. A geodesic is a curve that locally minimizes distance; it is the generalization of a "straight line" to a curved space.37 The curvature of a Riemannian manifold is a measure of how much its geometry deviates from that of flat Euclidean space. For example, on a sphere (positive curvature), the sum of angles in a triangle formed by geodesics is greater than 180 degrees, while on a hyperbolic plane (negative curvature), it is less.37 The machinery of Riemannian geometry is essential for the Nexus 4 Framework, as it provides the formal language to define the Semantic Field as a curved space whose local geometry (curvature) encodes semantic potential.381.2.3 Topological Data Analysis (TDA)While Riemannian geometry describes the local, metric properties of a space, topology describes its more fundamental, global properties of connection and shape that are invariant under continuous deformation. Topological Data Analysis (TDA) is a field that applies concepts from algebraic topology to analyze the "shape" of data.40 It is particularly useful for understanding the structure of point-cloud data, such as the set of word embeddings that might populate the Semantic Field.42The central tool in TDA is persistent homology.44 The persistent homology pipeline begins with a point cloud and a distance metric. It then constructs a sequence of simplicial complexes, which are higher-dimensional generalizations of graphs, at varying scales.46 A common construction is the Vietoris-Rips complex, where a simplex (a point, edge, triangle, tetrahedron, etc.) is formed by a set of points if the distance between every pair of points in the set is less than some scale parameter ϵ.46As the scale parameter ϵ increases, a nested sequence of simplicial complexes, called a filtration, is created.46 Persistent homology tracks the birth and death of topological features—connected components (0-dimensional holes), loops (1-dimensional holes), voids (2-dimensional holes), and so on—across this filtration. A feature is "born" at the scale ϵbirth​ where it first appears, and it "dies" at the scale ϵdeath​ where it is filled in or merges with a larger feature.44The results are summarized in a persistence diagram or barcode, which plots the birth-death pairs of all features.48 Features with a long persistence (a large difference between death and birth scales) are considered robust and significant, likely representing true structural aspects of the data, while features with short persistence are often treated as noise.45 TDA provides a powerful, scale-invariant method for characterizing the topology of the Semantic Field, identifying its fundamental structural components, such as conceptual clusters (connected components) or cyclical relationships between concepts (loops).491.3 The Dynamics of Interaction: Scattering TheoryIf the Semantic Field provides the static stage for cognition, we need a physical principle to describe the dynamic act of computing meaning. The Nexus 4 Framework proposes that this act is analogous to a scattering experiment in quantum mechanics.50 Scattering theory is a framework for studying and predicting the outcomes of collisions between particles.50 The core idea is to understand a localized object or potential by observing how an incident particle is deflected or transformed by it.50In a typical scattering experiment, an incident wave packet (a localized quantum particle) is prepared in a state of known momentum and sent towards a scattering potential (representing the target). After the interaction, the particle emerges as a scattered wave, which is a superposition of waves moving in various directions.50 The central object in scattering theory is the S-matrix (Scattering matrix), which is a unitary operator that maps the initial "in" state of the system (before the collision) to the final "out" state (after the collision).50 The elements of the S-matrix contain all the information about the interaction, including the probabilities of scattering into different final states. These probabilities are quantified by the scattering cross-section, which measures the effective "size" of the target for a given interaction process.51 The phase of the scattered wave relative to the incident wave, known as the phase shift, provides information about the nature (attractive or repulsive) of the potential.52This formalism provides a rich analogy for the computation of meaning. The Symbolic Probe is analogous to the incident wave packet, a structured entity prepared for interaction. The local geometry of the Semantic Field at the point of inquiry acts as the scattering potential. The process of "understanding" is the scattering event itself, and the "meaning" is the outcome—the properties of the scattered wave. A high degree of resonance or constructive interference, corresponding to a large scattering cross-section in a particular "channel," signifies a strong semantic match between the probe and the field. Recent work in computational physics has even begun to use neural network architectures, such as SwitchNet, to learn the complex forward and inverse maps in scattering problems, demonstrating the computational tractability of such models.531.4 The Dynamics of State: Phase Space and AttractorsTo fully describe the Symbolic Probe and its trajectory, we turn to the language of dynamical systems theory. A dynamical system is a system whose state evolves over time according to a fixed rule.55 The mathematical framework for describing such systems is phase space.57A phase space is an abstract space where every possible state of a system corresponds to a unique point.57 The dimensions of the phase space are the degrees of freedom of the system—the minimum number of variables required to specify its state completely.59 For a simple mechanical system like a pendulum, the phase space is two-dimensional, with axes for position and momentum.60 The evolution of the system over time is represented as a trajectory—a curve traced out by the state point as it moves through the phase space.57A key concept in dynamical systems is that of an attractor. An attractor is a subset of the phase space towards which the system's trajectories tend to evolve from a wide range of initial conditions.61 The set of all initial states that converge to a particular attractor is called its basin of attraction.62 Attractors can take several forms:A fixed point is a state that does not change over time; trajectories nearby may converge to it (a stable fixed point or sink).A limit cycle is a closed, periodic trajectory that attracts nearby trajectories.A strange attractor is an attractor with a fractal structure, on which the dynamics are chaotic, meaning they exhibit sensitive dependence on initial conditions.61This framework provides the formal language for modeling the Symbolic Probe. The probe's internal state is not static but is represented as a point in its own phase space. The process of inquiry, or thinking, is the evolution of this state—a trajectory through phase space. The hillclimb_anti_drift algorithm described in the computational experiments 77 can be seen as a search for an attractor (a state of high, stable resonance) in the probe's phase space.1.5 The Cognitive Grounding: Enactivism and Conceptual SpacesA computational physics of cognition must not only be mathematically and physically coherent but also plausible as a model of the mind. The Nexus 4 Framework finds its philosophical and cognitive grounding in two influential theories: the enactive approach to cognition and Gärdenfors' theory of conceptual spaces.The enactive approach is a paradigm in cognitive science that challenges the traditional view of the mind as a computer that processes internal representations of an external world.63 Instead, enactivism posits that cognition is an embodied activity that arises from the dynamic, reciprocal interaction between an autonomous, self-organizing agent and its environment.64 For enactivists, cognition is a process of "sense-making," where an agent "enacts" or brings forth a world of significance through its actions.63 This perspective rejects a sharp division between mind, body, and world, viewing them as an inseparable, dynamically coupled system.67 The Nexus 4 Framework aligns deeply with this view. The interaction between the Symbolic Probe (the agent) and the Semantic Field (the environment) is the central, irreducible event. Meaning is not represented in the field or in the probe but is enacted through their interaction. The CBE trajectory is a formal model of this process of sense-making, where the agent lays down a path of inquiry through its world.While enactivism provides the process-oriented philosophy, Peter Gärdenfors' theory of Conceptual Spaces provides a model for the structure of the cognitive world that is enacted.68 Gärdenfors proposes that concepts are not represented symbolically but as regions in a geometric space built from a set of "quality dimensions" (e.g., color, shape, temperature).70 A crucial thesis of this theory is that natural concepts correspond to convex regions in this space.68 A region is convex if for any two points within it, the entire line segment connecting them also lies within the region. This geometric property naturally gives rise to prototype effects, as some points will be more central to a region than others.68 The theory of conceptual spaces provides a compelling cognitive model for the structure of the Semantic Field. The low-potential valleys and basins of attraction within the field can be directly identified with the convex regions that Gärdenfors proposes as the geometric representation of natural concepts.The history of cognitive science and AI reveals a persistent tension between static, structural models of knowledge (like semantic networks or Gärdenfors' conceptual spaces) and dynamic, process-oriented models of knowing (like enactivism or dynamical systems theory). The former excel at describing what is known, while the latter focus on how knowing happens. A unifying framework has been elusive. The Nexus 4 Framework offers precisely this unification. The Semantic Field, with its Riemannian geometry and topological features, provides a formal basis for the structure of a conceptual space. The trajectory of the Symbolic Probe, governed by the CBE dynamic, provides a formal model for the process of enactive sense-making. These two aspects are not separate but are deeply coupled: the probe's dynamics are governed by the potential gradients of the field's geometry, and the field's geometry is, in turn, shaped by the history of these dynamic interactions through the scarring mechanism. This creates a fully integrated, geometro-dynamic model where the structure of knowledge and the process of cognition are two facets of the same underlying computational physics.Chapter 2: The Nexus 4 Framework: A Formal SpecificationHaving established the theoretical foundations, this chapter presents the formal specification of the Nexus 4 Framework. Adopting the principles of formal methods, this chapter treats the framework not as a loose collection of analogies but as a rigorously defined computational system.73 Formal methods employ mathematically-based techniques to describe and verify the properties of complex systems, aiming to provide a level of rigor that complements or surpasses empirical testing.61 The specification will be guided by the principles of Interface-Based Design, wherein the interactions between components are defined by a formal "contract" that specifies their inputs, outputs, and allowed behaviors.74 This approach ensures that the relationship between the Symbolic Probe and the Semantic Field is well-posed and verifiable. The following table serves as a concise summary and guide to the formal principles that constitute the framework, linking each conceptual pillar to its physical analogy, mathematical formalism, and its concrete computational instantiation in the experiments that will be detailed in Chapter 3.Nexus PrinciplePhysical AnalogyMathematical FormalismComputational Instantiation 77I. Semantic FieldDynamic Potential FieldRiemannian Manifold (M,g)cbe_sim field arrayII. Symbolic ProbeParticle in Phase SpaceState Vector (q,p)∈PS​hillclimb_anti_drift stateIII. Meaning ComputationResonant Scattering EventMapping I and observable phase_scoredigest_phase_score functionIV. CBE TrajectoryConstrained Gradient DescentConstrained Differential Equationhillclimb_anti_drift loop with branching2.1 Formal Methods and Interface-Based DesignFormal methods provide a means to model complex systems as mathematical entities, allowing their properties to be verified with a rigor that is unachievable through empirical testing alone.61 The process typically involves three stages: formal specification, verification, and implementation.61 In the formal specification phase, a system is defined using a modeling language with a precise mathematical grammar, akin to translating a word problem into algebraic notation. This specification can then be subjected to verification, where automated tools like model checkers or theorem provers are used to prove that the system adheres to desired properties (e.g., safety, liveness).73A key principle in designing complex, verifiable systems is Interface-Based Design. An interface can be understood as a formal contract between a system component and its environment.75 This contract specifies precisely what information can pass between the components and what behaviors are guaranteed. By defining clear interfaces, components can be developed and verified independently, a principle known as independent implementability, which greatly enhances modularity and maintainability.74 In the Nexus 4 Framework, the interaction between the Symbolic Probe and the Semantic Field is governed by such a formal interface, ensuring that their coupling is mathematically well-defined and their combined behavior is analyzable. The specification languages used in formal methods, such as Z, VDM, or TLA+, provide the logical and mathematical constructs needed to define these contracts with precision.802.2 Principle I: The Semantic Field as a Dynamic Potential ManifoldThe first principle of the Nexus 4 Framework posits that the substrate of knowledge is a dynamic, structured space.Formal Definition: The Semantic Field, denoted F, is formally defined as a Riemannian manifold (M,g). Here, M is a smooth, d-dimensional differentiable manifold that represents the space of all possible concepts, and g is a Riemannian metric tensor that defines the local geometry at every point p∈M.36Potential as Metric and Curvature: Unlike classical potential fields where potential is a separate scalar function defined on a static space, in the Semantic Field, the semantic potential is intrinsically encoded within the geometry of the space itself. The local potential at a point p is a function of the local properties of the metric tensor, gp​. Specifically, regions of low potential, corresponding to stable concepts or "valleys" in the semantic landscape, are characterized by specific curvature properties. A change in the semantic landscape is thus a change in the geometry of the manifold itself.Dynamics of the Field (Scarring and Decay): The Semantic Field is not static; it evolves based on its history of interactions.
